My mind just keeps jumping around—from my contract work to how I'm going to make the mortgage payments, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed by all the worrying, but it's not depression or sadness. It's just this constant, exhausting anxiety about everything falling apart. Um, this has all been going on since I was fired about thirteen months ago. That really set it off. And it just... it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. The worrying just stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. On the work side, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and... I've actually delayed sending out client deliverables. I just get completely stuck anticipating everything that could possibly go wrong with them, so I end up putting it off. \n\nAnd it's affecting my personal life, too. I’ve pulled back from playing soccer and seeing my friends. Honestly, I’m just embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve become around people, so it's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations because you're afraid of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really that. I'm not sitting there worrying that they're judging me or scrutinizing me. It's more just... well, like I said, I know how tense and irritable I get right now. I don't want to snap at my friends or bring the whole mood down. \n\nPlus, my brain is just so overloaded with all the other stuff I'm constantly worrying about. I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don't know, that I'll never get back to a real leadership role. It just spirals, and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts are spiraling out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's a really intense anxiety. My mind just keeps jumping around—from my contract work to how I'm going to make the mortgage payments, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. It gets to the point where... I don't know, just a delayed email from a recruiter can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yes, I feel very anxious when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really sad or down. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed by all the worrying, but it's not depression or sadness. It's just this constant, exhausting anxiety about everything falling apart. I’ve pulled back from playing soccer and seeing my friends. Honestly, I’m just embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve become around people, so it's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations because you're afraid of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really that. My mind just keeps jumping around—from my contract work to how I'm going to make the mortgage payments, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. So, yes, I feel very anxious when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really sad or down. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed by all the worrying, but it's not depression or sadness. It's just this constant, exhausting anxiety about everything falling apart. But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad.\n\nDoctor: Did these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety start after something significant happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. Um, this has all been going on since I was fired about thirteen months ago. That really set it off. And it just... it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. The worrying just stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. On the work side, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and... I've actually delayed sending out client deliverables. I just get completely stuck anticipating everything that could possibly go wrong with them, so I end up putting it off. \n\nAnd it's affecting my personal life, too. I’ve pulled back from playing soccer and seeing my friends. Honestly, I’m just embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve become around people, so it's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations because you're afraid of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really that. I'm not sitting there worrying that they're judging me or scrutinizing me. It's more just... well, like I said, I know how tense and irritable I get right now. I don't want to snap at my friends or bring the whole mood down. \n\nPlus, my brain is just so overloaded with all the other stuff I'm constantly worrying about. I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\nDoctor: Are these spiraling thoughts and anxiety triggered by reminders of your job loss or related situations?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm reminded of getting fired. It's just a constant loop in my head about everything—bills, my health, my family. It just spirals, and I can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when these thoughts are spiraling out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's a really intense anxiety. My mind just keeps jumping around—from my contract work to how I'm going to make the mortgage payments, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. It gets to the point where... I don't know, just a delayed email from a recruiter can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yes, I feel very anxious when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really sad or down. I mean, I get frustrated and overwhelmed by all the worrying, but it's not depression or sadness. It's just this constant, exhausting anxiety about everything falling apart. But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad.\n\nDoctor: Did these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety start after something significant happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. Um, this has all been going on since I was fired about thirteen months ago. That really set it off. And it just... it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. The worrying just stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these spiraling thoughts and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. On the work side, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and... I've actually delayed sending out client deliverables. I just get completely stuck anticipating everything that could possibly go wrong with them, so I end up putting it off. \n\nAnd it's affecting my personal life, too. I’ve pulled back from playing soccer and seeing my friends. Honestly, I’m just embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve become around people, so it's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations because you're afraid of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really that. I'm not sitting there worrying that they're judging me or scrutinizing me. It's more just... well, like I said, I know how tense and irritable I get right now. I don't want to snap at my friends or bring the whole mood down. \n\nPlus, my brain is just so overloaded with all the other stuff I'm constantly worrying about. I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\nDoctor: Are these spiraling thoughts and anxiety triggered by reminders of your job loss or related situations?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm reminded of getting fired. I mean, sure, work stuff stresses me out, but... the worrying happens all the time, even when there's no obvious trigger or reminder of the old job. It's just a constant loop in my head about everything—bills, my health, my family. It doesn't take a specific reminder to set it off; it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started right around the time I got fired from my old job. And... well, it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. It's just been going on ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or life changes other than your job loss in the past thirteen months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, getting fired was the big one. Other than that... no, nothing really stands out. \n\nThere haven't been any other major life changes or huge crises. No family emergencies, no moving, nothing like that. It was really just losing that job, and then... well, everything just sort of spiraled from there. But as far as other big events, no, there hasn't been anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods of several months or more in your life where you didn't worry about things as much as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I can't think of a specific time. It feels like I've always had some level of worry going on in the background. I don't really remember a stretch of several months where my mind was just... quiet, or where I wasn't stressing about something. So, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where your worrying and anxiety significantly decreased after you adapted to a stressful situation?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Even when things settle down, or I get used to whatever the new normal is... the anxiety doesn't really go away. \n\nLike with the contract work I have now, I figured I'd feel better once I was employed again, but... I don't. The worry just kind of shifts to something else. I just find a new thing to stress about. So, no, it never really decreases like that.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're not worried about something?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I really can't think of a time. There's... there's always something on my mind. If one problem actually gets solved, my brain just immediately finds the next thing to focus on. Even if I try to just sit down and relax, the thoughts are still there in the background. So, no, it never really shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Is it usually one specific thing that you're worried about, or is it a wide range of different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a wide range of things. My mind just... keeps jumping around. I'll be stressing about my contract work, and then it immediately shifts to the mortgage payments. Then I start worrying about my reputation, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. \n\nIt gets to the point where even just a delayed email from a recruiter... it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's never just one specific thing. It's always a bunch of different things at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying and anxiety would be better described as being about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that's exactly it. It really is about most things, most of the time. Like I was saying, it's just a constant cycle. My brain is always jumping from one thing to the next... whether it's the contract work, or making the mortgage, or worrying if everyone in the Bethesda tech scene thinks I'm washed up. It's just always there. Even something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back... my mind immediately goes to the worst-case scenario, like it's proof my career is totally over. So, yes, it's pretty much everything, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about everyday, routine things or is it more focused on specific, significant life events?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly everyday things, but they end up feeling like major life events. Like I was just saying, my mind just keeps jumping from regular, daily stuff—like the contract work or making the mortgage payments—to bigger things, like worrying if people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods. \n\nEven a totally routine thing, like a delayed email from a recruiter... I don't just see it as a normal delay. My brain turns it into this huge disaster, like it's absolute proof my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it starts with everyday things, but my anxiety makes them feel incredibly significant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

On the work side, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups, and... I've actually delayed sending out client deliverables. I just get completely stuck anticipating everything that could possibly go wrong with them, so I end up putting it off. \n\nAnd it's affecting my personal life, too. I’ve pulled back from playing soccer and seeing my friends. Honestly, I’m just embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve become around people, so it's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations because you're afraid of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really that. I'm not sitting there worrying that they're judging me or scrutinizing me. It's more just... well, like I said, I know how tense and irritable I get right now. I don't want to snap at my friends or bring the whole mood down. \n\nPlus, my brain is just so overloaded with all the other stuff I'm constantly worrying about. I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\nDoctor: Are these spiraling thoughts and anxiety triggered by reminders of your job loss or related situations?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm reminded of getting fired. I mean, sure, work stuff stresses me out, but... the worrying happens all the time, even when there's no obvious trigger or reminder of the old job. It's just a constant loop in my head about everything—bills, my health, my family. It doesn't take a specific reminder to set it off; it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started right around the time I got fired from my old job. And... well, it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. It's just been going on ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or life changes other than your job loss in the past thirteen months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, getting fired was the big one. Other than that... no, nothing really stands out. \n\nThere haven't been any other major life changes or huge crises. No family emergencies, no moving, nothing like that. It was really just losing that job, and then... well, everything just sort of spiraled from there. But as far as other big events, no, there hasn't been anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods of several months or more in your life where you didn't worry about things as much as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I can't think of a specific time. It feels like I've always had some level of worry going on in the background. I don't really remember a stretch of several months where my mind was just... quiet, or where I wasn't stressing about something. So, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where your worrying and anxiety significantly decreased after you adapted to a stressful situation?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Even when things settle down, or I get used to whatever the new normal is... the anxiety doesn't really go away. \n\nLike with the contract work I have now, I figured I'd feel better once I was employed again, but... I don't. The worry just kind of shifts to something else. I just find a new thing to stress about. So, no, it never really decreases like that.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're not worried about something?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I really can't think of a time. There's... there's always something on my mind. If one problem actually gets solved, my brain just immediately finds the next thing to focus on. Even if I try to just sit down and relax, the thoughts are still there in the background. So, no, it never really shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Is it usually one specific thing that you're worried about, or is it a wide range of different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a wide range of things. My mind just... keeps jumping around. I'll be stressing about my contract work, and then it immediately shifts to the mortgage payments. Then I start worrying about my reputation, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. \n\nIt gets to the point where even just a delayed email from a recruiter... it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's never just one specific thing. It's always a bunch of different things at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying and anxiety would be better described as being about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that's exactly it. It really is about most things, most of the time. Like I was saying, it's just a constant cycle. My brain is always jumping from one thing to the next... whether it's the contract work, or making the mortgage, or worrying if everyone in the Bethesda tech scene thinks I'm washed up. It's just always there. Even something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back... my mind immediately goes to the worst-case scenario, like it's proof my career is totally over. So, yes, it's pretty much everything, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about everyday, routine things or is it more focused on specific, significant life events?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly everyday things, but they end up feeling like major life events. Like I was just saying, my mind just keeps jumping from regular, daily stuff—like the contract work or making the mortgage payments—to bigger things, like worrying if people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods. \n\nEven a totally routine thing, like a delayed email from a recruiter... I don't just see it as a normal delay. My brain turns it into this huge disaster, like it's absolute proof my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it starts with everyday things, but my anxiety makes them feel incredibly significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your health as well?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's actually one of the few things I don't spend much time stressing over. I mean, physically I feel fine, so my brain doesn't really latch onto that. It's mostly just the work and the financial stuff I mentioned that I can't stop thinking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\nDoctor: Are these spiraling thoughts and anxiety triggered by reminders of your job loss or related situations?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm reminded of getting fired. I mean, sure, work stuff stresses me out, but... the worrying happens all the time, even when there's no obvious trigger or reminder of the old job. It's just a constant loop in my head about everything—bills, my health, my family. It doesn't take a specific reminder to set it off; it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started right around the time I got fired from my old job. And... well, it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. It's just been going on ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or life changes other than your job loss in the past thirteen months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, getting fired was the big one. Other than that... no, nothing really stands out. \n\nThere haven't been any other major life changes or huge crises. No family emergencies, no moving, nothing like that. It was really just losing that job, and then... well, everything just sort of spiraled from there. But as far as other big events, no, there hasn't been anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods of several months or more in your life where you didn't worry about things as much as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I can't think of a specific time. It feels like I've always had some level of worry going on in the background. I don't really remember a stretch of several months where my mind was just... quiet, or where I wasn't stressing about something. So, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where your worrying and anxiety significantly decreased after you adapted to a stressful situation?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Even when things settle down, or I get used to whatever the new normal is... the anxiety doesn't really go away. \n\nLike with the contract work I have now, I figured I'd feel better once I was employed again, but... I don't. The worry just kind of shifts to something else. I just find a new thing to stress about. So, no, it never really decreases like that.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're not worried about something?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I really can't think of a time. There's... there's always something on my mind. If one problem actually gets solved, my brain just immediately finds the next thing to focus on. Even if I try to just sit down and relax, the thoughts are still there in the background. So, no, it never really shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Is it usually one specific thing that you're worried about, or is it a wide range of different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a wide range of things. My mind just... keeps jumping around. I'll be stressing about my contract work, and then it immediately shifts to the mortgage payments. Then I start worrying about my reputation, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. \n\nIt gets to the point where even just a delayed email from a recruiter... it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's never just one specific thing. It's always a bunch of different things at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying and anxiety would be better described as being about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that's exactly it. It really is about most things, most of the time. Like I was saying, it's just a constant cycle. My brain is always jumping from one thing to the next... whether it's the contract work, or making the mortgage, or worrying if everyone in the Bethesda tech scene thinks I'm washed up. It's just always there. Even something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back... my mind immediately goes to the worst-case scenario, like it's proof my career is totally over. So, yes, it's pretty much everything, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about everyday, routine things or is it more focused on specific, significant life events?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly everyday things, but they end up feeling like major life events. Like I was just saying, my mind just keeps jumping from regular, daily stuff—like the contract work or making the mortgage payments—to bigger things, like worrying if people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods. \n\nEven a totally routine thing, like a delayed email from a recruiter... I don't just see it as a normal delay. My brain turns it into this huge disaster, like it's absolute proof my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it starts with everyday things, but my anxiety makes them feel incredibly significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your health as well?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's actually one of the few things I don't spend much time stressing over. I mean, physically I feel fine, so my brain doesn't really latch onto that. It's mostly just the work and the financial stuff I mentioned that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would have started or would be as severe if you hadn't lost your job thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I mean, losing the job definitely threw everything into high gear. But... if that hadn't happened, would it still be this bad? It's hard to say. I just don't know what it would look like otherwise.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\nDoctor: Are these spiraling thoughts and anxiety triggered by reminders of your job loss or related situations?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm reminded of getting fired. I mean, sure, work stuff stresses me out, but... the worrying happens all the time, even when there's no obvious trigger or reminder of the old job. It's just a constant loop in my head about everything—bills, my health, my family. It doesn't take a specific reminder to set it off; it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started right around the time I got fired from my old job. And... well, it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. It's just been going on ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or life changes other than your job loss in the past thirteen months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, getting fired was the big one. Other than that... no, nothing really stands out. \n\nThere haven't been any other major life changes or huge crises. No family emergencies, no moving, nothing like that. It was really just losing that job, and then... well, everything just sort of spiraled from there. But as far as other big events, no, there hasn't been anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods of several months or more in your life where you didn't worry about things as much as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I can't think of a specific time. It feels like I've always had some level of worry going on in the background. I don't really remember a stretch of several months where my mind was just... quiet, or where I wasn't stressing about something. So, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where your worrying and anxiety significantly decreased after you adapted to a stressful situation?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Even when things settle down, or I get used to whatever the new normal is... the anxiety doesn't really go away. \n\nLike with the contract work I have now, I figured I'd feel better once I was employed again, but... I don't. The worry just kind of shifts to something else. I just find a new thing to stress about. So, no, it never really decreases like that.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're not worried about something?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I really can't think of a time. There's... there's always something on my mind. If one problem actually gets solved, my brain just immediately finds the next thing to focus on. Even if I try to just sit down and relax, the thoughts are still there in the background. So, no, it never really shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Is it usually one specific thing that you're worried about, or is it a wide range of different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a wide range of things. My mind just... keeps jumping around. I'll be stressing about my contract work, and then it immediately shifts to the mortgage payments. Then I start worrying about my reputation, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. \n\nIt gets to the point where even just a delayed email from a recruiter... it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's never just one specific thing. It's always a bunch of different things at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying and anxiety would be better described as being about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that's exactly it. It really is about most things, most of the time. Like I was saying, it's just a constant cycle. My brain is always jumping from one thing to the next... whether it's the contract work, or making the mortgage, or worrying if everyone in the Bethesda tech scene thinks I'm washed up. It's just always there. Even something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back... my mind immediately goes to the worst-case scenario, like it's proof my career is totally over. So, yes, it's pretty much everything, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about everyday, routine things or is it more focused on specific, significant life events?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly everyday things, but they end up feeling like major life events. Like I was just saying, my mind just keeps jumping from regular, daily stuff—like the contract work or making the mortgage payments—to bigger things, like worrying if people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods. \n\nEven a totally routine thing, like a delayed email from a recruiter... I don't just see it as a normal delay. My brain turns it into this huge disaster, like it's absolute proof my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it starts with everyday things, but my anxiety makes them feel incredibly significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your health as well?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's actually one of the few things I don't spend much time stressing over. I mean, physically I feel fine, so my brain doesn't really latch onto that. It's mostly just the work and the financial stuff I mentioned that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would have started or would be as severe if you hadn't lost your job thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I mean, losing the job definitely threw everything into high gear. But... if that hadn't happened, would it still be this bad? It's hard to say. I just don't know what it would look like otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. That's actually why I came in... I just can't shut my brain off anymore. It's like I have no control over it. It starts with just one small concern—like a client not responding to an email—and within minutes, my mind just runs with it. I'm suddenly convinced I'm going to lose the contract, and then I'm worrying about falling behind on my divorce payments, and then I'm thinking I'll never get back into a real leadership role again. It just spirals, and I can't seem to stop it once it starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Honestly, I’m just embarrassed by how tense and irritable I’ve become around people, so it's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations because you're afraid of what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really that. I'm not sitting there worrying that they're judging me or scrutinizing me. It's more just... well, like I said, I know how tense and irritable I get right now. I don't want to snap at my friends or bring the whole mood down. \n\nPlus, my brain is just so overloaded with all the other stuff I'm constantly worrying about. I just don't have the energy to go out and try to put on a happy face. So it's not really about what they think of me, it's just... I just can't handle being around people right now.\n\nDoctor: Are these spiraling thoughts and anxiety triggered by reminders of your job loss or related situations?\n\nPatient: No, it's not just when I'm reminded of getting fired. I mean, sure, work stuff stresses me out, but... the worrying happens all the time, even when there's no obvious trigger or reminder of the old job. It's just a constant loop in my head about everything—bills, my health, my family. It doesn't take a specific reminder to set it off; it's just always there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these spiraling thoughts and intense anxiety?\n\nPatient: It's been about thirteen months now. It started right around the time I got fired from my old job. And... well, it never really let up, even after I managed to find the contract work I'm doing now. It's just been going on ever since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or life changes other than your job loss in the past thirteen months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, getting fired was the big one. Other than that... no, nothing really stands out. \n\nThere haven't been any other major life changes or huge crises. No family emergencies, no moving, nothing like that. It was really just losing that job, and then... well, everything just sort of spiraled from there. But as far as other big events, no, there hasn't been anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods of several months or more in your life where you didn't worry about things as much as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I can't think of a specific time. It feels like I've always had some level of worry going on in the background. I don't really remember a stretch of several months where my mind was just... quiet, or where I wasn't stressing about something. So, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time where your worrying and anxiety significantly decreased after you adapted to a stressful situation?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Even when things settle down, or I get used to whatever the new normal is... the anxiety doesn't really go away. \n\nLike with the contract work I have now, I figured I'd feel better once I was employed again, but... I don't. The worry just kind of shifts to something else. I just find a new thing to stress about. So, no, it never really decreases like that.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you're not worried about something?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I really can't think of a time. There's... there's always something on my mind. If one problem actually gets solved, my brain just immediately finds the next thing to focus on. Even if I try to just sit down and relax, the thoughts are still there in the background. So, no, it never really shuts off.\n\nDoctor: Is it usually one specific thing that you're worried about, or is it a wide range of different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a wide range of things. My mind just... keeps jumping around. I'll be stressing about my contract work, and then it immediately shifts to the mortgage payments. Then I start worrying about my reputation, like whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. \n\nIt gets to the point where even just a delayed email from a recruiter... it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's never just one specific thing. It's always a bunch of different things at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying and anxiety would be better described as being about most things most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that's exactly it. It really is about most things, most of the time. Like I was saying, it's just a constant cycle. My brain is always jumping from one thing to the next... whether it's the contract work, or making the mortgage, or worrying if everyone in the Bethesda tech scene thinks I'm washed up. It's just always there. Even something small, like a recruiter taking too long to email back... my mind immediately goes to the worst-case scenario, like it's proof my career is totally over. So, yes, it's pretty much everything, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about everyday, routine things or is it more focused on specific, significant life events?\n\nPatient: I think it's mostly everyday things, but they end up feeling like major life events. Like I was just saying, my mind just keeps jumping from regular, daily stuff—like the contract work or making the mortgage payments—to bigger things, like worrying if people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods. \n\nEven a totally routine thing, like a delayed email from a recruiter... I don't just see it as a normal delay. My brain turns it into this huge disaster, like it's absolute proof my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it starts with everyday things, but my anxiety makes them feel incredibly significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about your health as well?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That's actually one of the few things I don't spend much time stressing over. I mean, physically I feel fine, so my brain doesn't really latch onto that. It's mostly just the work and the financial stuff I mentioned that I can't stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would have started or would be as severe if you hadn't lost your job thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure, to be honest. I mean, losing the job definitely threw everything into high gear. But... if that hadn't happened, would it still be this bad? It's hard to say. I just don't know what it would look like otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. That's actually why I came in... I just can't shut my brain off anymore. It's like I have no control over it. It starts with just one small concern—like a client not responding to an email—and within minutes, my mind just runs with it. I'm suddenly convinced I'm going to lose the contract, and then I'm worrying about falling behind on my divorce payments, and then I'm thinking I'll never get back into a real leadership role again. It just spirals, and I can't seem to stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying would have decreased significantly if you had not lost your job thirteen months ago?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. It's hard to guess how things would have turned out. Like I said, losing the job definitely made everything worse, but I can't say for sure if the worrying would have gone away or even decreased if I was still working there. I just don't have a good answer for that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
